> From the very beginning of the conflict, it was obvious that Russia's actions were wrong. I often argued with those who supported her on the Internet. Although Trump was not an ideal candidate, compared to Biden and Harris, he looked like almost the only alternative. And most importantly, he promised to end the war.
  
> 
  
> The businessification of Ukraine was actively discussed on the Internet, while the situation in Russia remained calm. The negotiations between Zelensky and Trump were the last straw for the anti-war movement.
  
> 
  
> I didn't understand politics until 2022, but I started studying it and realized that I was right-wing, even though initially I was just an anti-war liberal. But not everyone became right—wing - some began to associate themselves with Democrats and became leftists. It's like a meme from Fast and Furious, where two people went their separate ways. And as a result, the principle "the enemy of my enemy is my friend" arises, although this is a logical mistake. I understand those who have changed their views on the Russian-Ukrainian conflict. The political struggle in the United States is too comprehensive, it defines reality. People start thinking in two different realities, and in fact, people are more likely to defend their culture or way of thinking than comfort.

My respect for your analyses, professor. I follow you with great interest!
Regarding the subject of the analysis: I think that Putin would not fight with Turkey to conquer Constantinople. A Turkey-Russia war is possible if Turkey initiates it.
The Orthodox Church is important to Putin for its moral-conservative values that oppose the woke ideology.
Putin wants families with traditional values and many children, respectively a strong people that can ensure the future of the Russian Federation.
The Orthodox Christian religion could be a binder for areas that would choose to be under Russian influence for similar reasons: the defense of traditional moral values.
How I see the geopolitical situation:
1. The USA has major internal economic and social problems, a major Russia-Europe war would suit America, in the hope that it would indirectly solve the American crisis. This war could start with the battle for Odessa and would involve, first of all, the countries bordering Ukraine.
(In my opinion, it is a trap for the EU and would degenerate into the use of a medium-sized nuclear bomb.)
2. Ukraine has two internal political positions: one that wants victory in the war, but also a less visible political opposition, opposed to Western interests. This political opposition believes that the total conquest of Ukraine by the Russians would cancel all war debts and other economic losses of Ukraine. If this political opposition were to overthrow the government in Kiev, what effects would it have on the war, how would NATO act, how would the Ukrainian people react? It would be interesting to analyze this possibility.
3. I do not believe in the deterioration of the Russia-China alliance, it is a relationship of existential importance for both countries. And both countries will defend Iran because it is very important for the BRICS organization.

I look forward to your next analysis!
Have a nice week!

> @archi.enemigo to go back to Putin, since his family knew very well where the true power was - he went on to become a KGB officer. When the USSR collapsed it triggered a separation into warring factions: ex-Party politicians, local crime families, KGB and army bosses. It was a free for all. One of the most prominent 'reformer' politicians was the new mayor of Leningrad - Sobchak. By old Russian-Soviet tradition he had a KGB officer sent to join his office, the biggest mystery in the whole story is why Sobchak agreed since he was running on reform platform, anyway that officer was of course young Putin (this was 1990). By all accounts Putin was really good at his job: he was thorough, hard-working, loyal, attentive, good listener, was willing to carry out any tasks, never spilled any secrets and very importantly didn't seem to have any ambitions of his own. He quickly rose to importance and became the most trusted clerk, always in his boss' shadow: just google "Putin Sobchak", he's always somewhere behind his boss carrying his briefcase. Sobchak became one of the top 5 politicians in the country (behind President Yeltsin, Prime Minister Chernomyrdyn and Mayor of Moscow Luzhkov) which gave Putin (who was pretty much Sobchak's right-hand man at this point) access to every insider and political elite. Quite often Putin would communicate with them more than Sobchak himself. Yeltsin and Sobchak were semi-allies while other oligarchs to the East (who were often richer because of access to oil) wanted to get their political rivals in Moscow and Leningrad/Peterburg  
> Anyway, Yeltsin was becoming extremely unpopular and his days were almost done, but he couldn't just leave the office because other oligarchs would take everything from him and his family. Sobchak would be the best choice for him but as an extremely ambitious man (and also implicated in numerous corruption scandals at this point) he was hated even more by the other oligarchs who wouldn't agree. So as a compromise Sobchak's quiet 'unambitious technocrat' was to become the Prime Minister and Yeltsin's successor. This way Yeltisn and Sobchak's family fortunes would remain safe, while the other oligarchs believed that a timid clerk with no political support would be an extremely easy figure to control.  
> As soon as he gained power Putin unleashed his ambitions, destroyed free press, gained that political support, destroyed those oligarchs gaining even more political support and eventually became a dictator for life.  
>   
> This used to be open knowledge with government officials coming to the talk shows on TV, journalists investigating political deals and politicians writing books. This all stopped once Putin gained control of the media, but the people who witnessed it like me, we still remember it well.

There are 2 battlefield doctrines that predominate in the world .One is blitzkrieg which in its modern from is known as shock and awe .And the other is deep battle which is used by the Russians .The deep battle doctrine requires the blending of operational ,strategic and tactical doctrines into one unified doctrine .Shock and awe requires tactical knowledge but not operational . This why the Wehrmacht was finally defeated in the East .Operation Bagration stands as the shining example of the success of deep battle over blitzkrieg .The fact that Ukrainian forces can inflict damage to the Russians on a tactical level is irrelevant as in the end the Russian operational advantage will end in a terrible Ukrainian defeat . ### **@RaptorLlama** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZgvAHZqaawA&lc=UgzXTo2HywkMSZiVMCl4AaABAg)

Some thoughts from an Orthodox Christian familiar with these matters:

1. St. Paisios the Athonite is the source of some prophecies, but the one about Erdogan falling is from a Cretan monk in 2016. St. Paisios mostly didn't give new prophecies but interpreted older prophecies, largely of St. Kosmas (who lived in the 18th century), and of some anonymous athonites. There are also mainly fake prophecies around, but I think you generally identified attested ones. The main weight of them is that St. Kosmas made prophecies that appeared to accurately predict events in WWI and WWII, as well as key 20th century inventions, from the 18th century (or early 19th at most, but I think he died in the 18th).

2. The Russian Church, I would say, has two streams of thought within it. One does see Stalin and Putin in the light you depicted. The other is much more critical of both, the latter less overtly so due to the consequences inherent in that. The latter stems from the Catacomb Church that rejected the declaration of Metropolitan Sergius in the 1920s. They condemn Stalin and see his laxing on persecution as a political necessity to increase morale for the war. Some in this stream still see the prophecies as legitimate, but they I believe would be more skeptical of Putin and the general Russia glorification of the USSR, which many of the catcacomb saints saw as either a precusor or the very regime of Antichrist. Thus, there are some nuances.

It is an interesting hypothesis however and it is likely a good explanation of how Putin ascended to power and how he views himself, and it aligns with the neo-sergianist stream of thought in the Russian Church. And that stream does have ties with Mt. Athos, although I do not think they are so aligned with the Athonites that produced, held, and interpreted those prophecies (there are different factions on Mt. Athos as well).

### **@foreveryone1185**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZgvAHZqaawA&lc=UgwY_9xmfbGgVaqOwGN4AaABAg)

Your analytics are interesting, but you overlooked a crucial factor: the composition of the Russian military. If Russia were to provoke a war involving Turkey, it could face significant internal problems. A substantial portion—perhaps 30–50%—of Russian soldiers come from Turkic-speaking or Muslim-majority regions such as Tatarstan, Bashkortostan, Chechnya, and Dagestan. These groups may not support a war against a Muslim-majority country like Turkey, especially if the conflict escalates or appears unjustified.

This could lead to a serious breakdown in military cohesion. Russia's Slavic and non-Muslim population alone may not be enough to sustain a large-scale, multi-front war. Even if Ukraine is eventually subdued, Russia could face internal unrest or even civil conflict, especially if soldiers from minority groups begin to resist or defect.

Another critical issue is the sustainability of such a war. How long can Russia continue fighting Ukraine? How would it manage a second front against NATO or Turkey? President Putin is now 72 years old. What happens if he dies or becomes incapable of leading? Can the Russian elite maintain his aggressive foreign policy and centralized control? And can the Russian economy bear the burden of prolonged war, sanctions, and isolation?

There are many open questions that cast doubt on Russia’s long-term ability to maintain such a confrontation. So while some of your points are valid, it's important to consider the internal risks and structural weaknesses that could undermine Russia’s war efforts from within.

The biggest question is  if Vladimir Putin dies what will happen? Can Elite take control? Or will they battle between each other to get Putin's place? Or in this chaos every 21 Republics will try to get theis independent?

### **@positivebribes**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZgvAHZqaawA&lc=Ugx7tJIPfIf2iiCuWx94AaABAg)

For my own reference:

The speaker uses counterfactuals to illustrate how different outcomes might have occurred if certain American policies had not been implemented (2:22). He contrasts these with the actual events and then presents a hypothesis that leads to three predictions.

Here's a breakdown:

1. Counterfactuals vs. Reality:

Ukrainian Military Strategy (2:30-7:08):
Counterfactual (2:36): If the U.S. and NATO had not intervened, Russia would have quickly taken eastern Ukraine and encircled Odessa within 6 months. However, Russian overextension and poor logistics would have allowed Ukraine to launch effective guerrilla attacks, demoralizing Russian soldiers and turning Russian public opinion against the war. In this scenario, Ukraine would likely have won the war.
Reality (4:49): Ukraine chose to fight for every inch of territory, which played into Russia's strength in defensive artillery warfare (4:58). This constant engagement forced the Russian military to adapt and become more resilient, learning to use drone warfare (5:26). The Ukrainian military, seemingly run from Brussels or Washington, didn't adapt, leading to Russia gaining battlefield dominance (6:06) and Ukraine losing the war (7:06).

American Sanctions against Russia (7:18-11:13):
Counterfactual (7:36): If America had not imposed sanctions, the Russian oligarchs and business elite, seeing their industries suffer, would have become the biggest opposition to Putin (7:47), engaging in economic sabotage (8:10). This would have made it difficult for Putin to sustain the war.
Reality (8:26): By targeting the Russian business elite, America inadvertently unified them around Putin (8:34). Additionally, freezing $300 billion in Russian assets (10:45) undermined the credibility of the Western financial system (10:56) and the US dollar (11:03), which is seen as self-defeating for American imperial power.

Blowing up the Nord Stream Pipeline (11:16-13:07):
Counterfactual (11:33): If America had not blown up the pipeline, as the war continued, the German people would have naturally blamed Russia for their economic misery and stopped buying Russian gas, switching to American oil. The blame for economic hardship would have been on Putin.
Reality (12:18): By blowing up the pipeline, America caused the German economy to suffer, leading the German people to blame the U.S. for their economic misery (12:28). This action is seen as unleashing structural forces that could lead to an alliance between Germany and Russia (12:56), potentially destroying the Anglo-American hegemony.

2. The Hypothesis and Three Predictions:

The speaker's hypothesis is that a secret nexus of power (39:08) within the Orthodox Church and the KGB (24:49) has been nurturing Putin. This group believes in the "Third Rome prophecy" (21:48) and aims to restore the Byzantine Empire (25:30) by seeing Putin as the "second coming of Stalin" (24:30). The war in Ukraine is seen as an opportunity to destroy Western civilization and achieve these ambitions (39:40-39:48).

From this hypothesis, the speaker makes three predictions (39:53-42:35):

1.  Odessa will be a cataclysmic battle between NATO and Russia (40:04): The war in Ukraine will shift to Odessa, where NATO will make its final stand. This will lead to the implosion of the Turkish government and a revolution, allowing Russia to control Turkey and fulfill the prophecy of restoring the Byzantine Empire.
2.  China and Russia will have a falling out (40:36): If Russia is fighting a war for God and the soul of humanity, then China, being a "godless, valueless, materialistic society," is also an enemy. Russia will ultimately have contempt for China, leading to a quick falling out between them.
3.  Russia will defend Iran (41:33): Russia cannot afford for Iran to fall. In this geopolitical and cosmological view, Iran is a crucial ally for Russia in its fight to bring God back to humanity.

### **@fabiangunther6275**

As a German, I find the idea of a German-Russian alliance to be unrealistic; however, perhaps this perspective is clearer from beyond the Atlantic.



I would also like to point out that, from Nietzsche's viewpoint, Putin certainly would not qualify as an Übermensch—at least not for the reason you provided, namely the completion of a volkisch civilizational mission. The concept of the Übermensch is more closely linked to Christian mysticism as expressed by authors like Meister Eckhart and Thomas Aquinas, where it carries a personal connotation aimed at discovering one's true self through the renunciation and withdrawal from the world. Additionally, the reference to Hegel’s spirit or world spirit seems misplaced in this context.



What you present in this video can hardly be considered a hypothesis; it is rather a well-founded doctrine rooted in the Western intellectual tradition. Your argumentation appears more as a loose tapestry of ideas that does not rise to the level of a robust thesis.



In some ways, I wish for greater integrity and a stronger sense of responsibility from our so-called intellectual elite.

### **@kadenchin-massey5529**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZgvAHZqaawA&lc=UgywpOpyeKICAXImYQl4AaABAg)

Great video! However, I have a counter argument to make using some of your own logic from previous videos.

In your episode about Alexander the Great in the "Civilization" series, you argue that armies win not because of their size, technology, or resources but because of cohesion, discipline, and devotion. You say the true deciding factors in war are that the victors must be united, well-trained, and deeply committed to their cause.

I believe your analysis in this Ukraine video contradicts that logic. Ukraine is fighting to defend its homeland, which naturally builds national unity and a shared sense of purpose. Its soldiers are motivated by the survival of their nation and the future of their people. That kind of devotion produces morale, resilience, and a willingness to sacrifice, which often matters more than sheer numbers or equipment.

Russia by contrast lacks those same qualities. Its forces are often fighting for money, coercion, or the promise of personal freedom. The military relies heavily on conscripts, prisoners, and mercenaries with little shared identity or belief in the war's purpose. I believe this leads to serious problems with morale, discipline, and loyalty, the same weaknesses you pointed out in the Persian army when comparing it to Alexander's.

I have used AI to help me write this, however I hope this has not detracting from my argument because it is what I truly believe. This is also not even mentioning how Ukraine's use of drones has been impacting this war recently. And how with Russia's daily shaheed and ballistic attacks in Ukraine is only strengthening morale and the belief that Ukrainians are fighting a just cause. Would love and welcome a response to this argument.

### **@oliviabaklaton4552**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZgvAHZqaawA&lc=Ugy7e_z70nSp1wVhRjh4AaABAg)

Sorry to say: he is totally wrong concerning   Stalin and the orthodox church.
Here the facts.
No, the Orthodox Church as an institution did not support Stalin or his ideology. For most of his rule, the Soviet state, led by Stalin, carried out the most severe and violent persecution of Christianity in history. However, during World War II, a dramatic and pragmatic shift occurred where Stalin temporarily eased persecutions and formed an alliance of convenience with the Church to boost patriotic morale. The Church leadership, in turn, gave its full support to the war effort against Nazi Germany.

To understand this, it's best to break it down chronologically:

1. The Period of Extreme Persecution (1920s - Late 1930s)

· Official Atheism: The Soviet state was founded on Marxist-Leninist atheism, which viewed religion as a hostile "opiate of the masses" that needed to be eradicated.
· Stalin's Intensification: Under Stalin, this persecution reached its peak during the "Great Purge" and the broader "Anti-Religious Campaign."
· Actions Taken:
  · Execution and Imprisonment: Tens of thousands of clergy, monks, and nuns were executed or died in the Gulag prison camps. Among them were prominent bishops and theologians.
  · Destruction of Churches: Cathedrals and churches were systematically destroyed, desecrated, or converted into warehouses, barns, museums of atheism, or factories. The famous Christ the Saviour Cathedral in Moscow was dynamited on Stalin's orders in 1931.
  · Propaganda: Relentless anti-religious propaganda was spread through schools, media, and the League of the Militant Godless.

By 1939, the Russian Orthodox Church was decimated. Its institutional structure was nearly annihilated; only a few bishops remained free, and a tiny fraction of pre-revolutionary churches were still open.

2. The Pragmatic Wartime Alliance (1941-1945)

The relationship changed drastically with Operation Barbarossa—Nazi Germany's invasion of the Soviet Union in 1941.

· Stalin's Motivations (The State's Pivot):
  · National Unity: Stalin needed to rally the entire Soviet population, the vast majority of whom were still culturally Orthodox, to defend the motherland (Rodina). Attacking the Church would undermine this unity.
  · Patriotic Propaganda: The Church was a powerful symbol of Russian history and national identity. Stalin sought to tap into this deep well of patriotism to boost morale at the front and at home.
  · International Image: With allies like Britain and the USA, softening the stance on religion improved the USSR's image.
· The Church's Motivations (The Church's Pivot):
  · Survival: This was a chance for the Church to emerge from the catacombs and regain a legal existence.
  · Patriotism: Church leaders were genuinely patriotic and saw the Nazi invasion as an existential threat to the Russian people and their homeland—a "Holy War" to defend the faith and nation.
  · Spiritual Duty: To provide spiritual comfort to a nation undergoing unimaginable suffering.

Key Events of the Alliance:

· Meeting with Metropolitans: In September 1943, Stalin met with three surviving metropolitans (Sergius, Alexy, and Nicholas) in the Kremlin. It was the first such meeting since the revolution.
· Concessions Granted: Stalin agreed to:
  · Reopen theological academies and seminaries.
  · Allow the election of a Patriarch (the first since 1925). Metropolitan Sergius was elected Patriarch shortly after the meeting.
  · Permit the reopening of some churches.
  · Form a council for managing church affairs (The Council for the Affairs of the Russian Orthodox Church).
· Church Support: In return, the Church:
  · Encouraged enlistment and resistance against the Nazis.
  · Collected massive financial donations from the faithful to fund tank divisions and air squadrons (e.g., the Dmitry Donskoy tank column).
  · Provided powerful propaganda that framed the war not as a fight for communism, but for the salvation of Christian civilization.

3. The Post-War Period

After the war, the uneasy alliance continued, but the persecution never fully stopped.

· The Church was praised for its wartime role and allowed to maintain its reopened institutions.
· However, it remained tightly controlled by the KGB. Priests were often coerced into becoming informants.
· Widespread persecution and anti-religious campaigns resumed, though never with the same ferocity as the 1930s. The Church's public role was severely limited.

Conclusion: Did the Church "Support" Stalin?

· Ideologically? Absolutely not. Stalin's regime murdered countless Orthodox believers and sought to destroy the Church.
· Pragmatically during WWII? Yes. The Church leadership patriotically supported the national war effort against a foreign invader. In doing so, they accepted Stalin as the leader of that national defense.

### **@wiseturtleoncesaid** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZgvAHZqaawA&lc=UgxnlJ3Aaiu-ZlE_mUN4AaABAg)

As a Turkish International Relations graduate trying to look at things as objectively as possible, I wanted to write a few brief points for friends who don’t really know how Turkish foreign policy is shaped, so it’s clearer why what the professor is suggesting isn’t practically feasible:

1. First of all, Istanbul is an extremely sacred place not only for Orthodox Christians but also for Muslims. There are even sayings attributed to the Prophet Muhammad about how blessed the soldier would be who conquers it. In a country with a Sunni Muslim majority (and where the idea of martyrdom exists not only among Shiites but also among Sunnis), this makes the “capture” of a city like Istanbul basically impossible.


2. Turks are extremely nationalist, and things have reached the point where they could end up at war with Greece even over large rocks in the Aegean Sea and who they belong to. In fact, Turkey's domestic politics are shaped around the issue of protecting territorial integrity. The majority of the public is quite sensitive on this issue, and Turkish politicians tailor their rhetoric accordingly.


3. Even if Russia doesn’t “win” this war, the idea that a coup and civil disobedience would overthrow the government, and that in that chaotic environment someone would come and place a piece of land with a population of 16 million, with no shared language or religion, under their own protection, is a very unrealistic scenario.


4. If a coup were to happen, a military administration in Turkey would take control, but they wouldn’t simply hand over Istanbul either. This kind of military rule happened frequently before the 2000s, and it’s not something the public is unfamiliar with.

5. For Turkey, which no matter how pressured it was resisted entering even World War II until the very end, joining a war is unlikely unless there is a clear border violation. Even then, a balancing policy would be maintained as long as possible. Turkish political history has shown that aligning with only one side tends to create weakness in the long run.

The USA had been supplying weapons for several years before the SMO began, preparing Ukraine for conflict and creating public opinion against Russia. This thesis is supported by the objective strength of the Russian economy, which not only withstands sanctions but is also growing.

My theory about why Putin is doing all of this: he wants to be stopped. He wants to stop the war in Ukraine because, obviously, it didn’t go as expected (and the expectation really was that it would be easy – “three days,” as all the Russian propaganda said in the first days of the war). But he can’t stop it by himself, because he would look like a loser< that he lost the war. And he doesn’t want to be seen that way, and he can’t afford that. So he is throwing everything he can at Ukraine (meaning “at the West”), waiting for the West to finally respond in such a way that he can say to the Russians: “See? It’s too dangerous, and I don’t want you to die, so I will stop this to prevent that.” That way, he would not look like a loser, but a savior. But the West can’t do anything to provoke Putin into taking such a step, his decisions are too weak. So Putin is forced to appear increasingly crazy and do increasingly crazy things to provoke the West into taking decisive action. If this theory is correct, Putin will continue acting with increasing brazenness secretly hoping that the West will finally decide to do something more serious and then he, relieved, will stop all this and go rest.

And the mystic part of all this – about two or so years ago, I had a dream where I saw Putin (unexpectedly, I never thought I would have this), and there I felt exactly that – that he WANTS to be stopped.

Because he can't stop all of this by himself.
